But I’m not sure if I’d call it guilt exactly, or if it’s more that I’m confused and frustrated that it happened again.\n\nIt’s hard because in the moment I’m not really analyzing it, and afterward I’m sort of just sitting there aware that it was a lot. So… maybe there’s some self-judgment in there, but I don’t know if “guilty” is the exact word I’d use.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever do anything to try to make up for or counteract the extra calories you consume on those late nights?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that, because I don’t know exactly what would “count,” if that makes sense. Like, afterward I’m usually more in this mode of thinking, okay, that was too much, I wish I hadn’t done that, and then I sort of mentally promise myself I’ll be more normal about food the next day.\n\nBut I haven’t really kept track of whether I’m actively doing something to balance it out, versus just feeling uncomfortable and wanting to reset somehow. I think I’d need you to clarify what kinds of things you mean, because I don’t want to say yes or no too quickly and give the wrong impression.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after one of these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think about that carefully, because I don’t want to answer too automatically. I don’t really have a specific instance coming to mind where I’ve, like, intentionally done that afterward. Mostly after those nights I’m just sitting with the discomfort and thinking too much about what just happened.\n\nBut I also feel a little thrown by the question, because it’s very direct, and I’m realizing I haven’t really been categorizing my reactions afterward in that kind of way. So… I don’t think that’s something I’ve noticed myself doing, no.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever exercise more than usual the day after one of these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I don’t think I have a clear pattern where the next day I deliberately go, “Okay, I need to exercise because of what I ate last night.” That doesn’t really sound like something I’ve been consciously doing.\n\nBut I do sometimes have that feeling the next day of wanting to be more “on track,” if that makes sense, and I might think vaguely that I should move more or be healthier. I just don’t know that it turns into actual extra exercise. I’m not someone who’s tracking it closely enough to say, like, yes, every time this happens I go work out harder the next morning.\n\nSo… I haven’t really noticed that, no. At least not in a way that feels intentional or consistent.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over time due to these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m honestly not totally sure. I don’t weigh myself very consistently, partly because it can make me feel worse or get kind of stuck in my head about it, so I don’t have a clear before-and-after number I can point to.\n\nI do sometimes feel like my body changes a little, or like my clothes fit differently at certain times, but I can’t say confidently that it’s because of the late-night eating episodes specifically. It’s more like I have this general worry that they must be affecting my weight, and then I’ll feel upset about that, but I haven’t tracked it in a way where I could say, “Yes, I gained this much because of those nights.”\n\nSo… I guess the most accurate answer is that I haven’t noticed a clear, significant weight change that I can definitely connect to them.\n\nDoctor: During those late-night eating episodes, do you feel like you're able to stop eating when you want to, or do you feel like you can't stop once you've started?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that part is probably the thing that bothers me the most, honestly. It doesn’t feel like I’m just deciding to have a snack and then deciding to stop. At the beginning, sometimes I can tell myself it’s just going to be one thing, or I’m just going to have a little bit, but then once I’ve started there’s this point where it feels like something shifts.\n\nLike I’ll be sitting there and I know I should stop. I’m not confused about it. I’m literally thinking, “Okay, stop now, you don’t actually need more,” but then I’m still opening something else, or going back to get more, or clicking back into an app to look at food again. It’s almost like I’m watching myself do it from the side, if that makes sense.\n\nAnd I don’t want to make it sound like I’m unconscious or anything. I know what I’m doing. But the decision doesn’t feel fully available to me in that moment. It feels like by the time I realize it’s happening, it’s already kind of… running on its own, and stopping feels much harder than it should.\n\nDoctor: How often do these late-night eating episodes happen?\n\nPatient: It’s been happening pretty regularly, which is hard to say out loud because I think I was kind of telling myself it was more random than it is.\n\nWhen I actually look back at things like delivery receipts, or even just my calendar and what certain nights looked like, there’s usually at least one night every week where it happens. Sometimes it’s more than that. It’s not every single night, but it’s also not like once in a while anymore.\n\nAnd that pattern has been going on for several months, I think. I didn’t really notice it as a “pattern” at first because each time felt like its own separate bad night, but when I look back, it’s pretty consistent.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating once you've started every time one of these late-night eating episodes happens?\n\nPatient: Usually, yes. I’m trying to think if there are exceptions, because I don’t want to exaggerate it and say every single time is exactly the same. There are probably nights where it’s smaller or where I sort of interrupt it earlier.\n\nBut when I think of the episodes I’m actually worried about—the ones I would count as the late-night eating episodes—then yes, the loss of control is kind of the defining part of it. Once I start, there’s this point where I’m watching myself keep going back, like clicking back to the food app, or opening another package, or checking what else is there, even while I’m thinking, “You should stop now.” It’s not like I don’t know I should stop. I do know. That’s what makes it feel so awful.\n\nIt’s more like the decision has already slipped out of my hands by then. I can have the thought to stop, but it doesn’t really translate into stopping. And then afterward I’m like, why didn’t I just close the app or put the food away? But in the moment it feels much harder than that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these late-night eating episodes are causing you a lot of distress or anxiety?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer the “a lot” part. It definitely bothers me, and I don’t feel good about it, especially afterward. But I don’t know if I’d call it anxiety exactly, at least not every time.\n\nIn the moment, I’m usually not sitting there thinking, “I’m anxious.” It’s more like I’m caught up in it, and then afterward I’m uncomfortable with what happened and kind of replay it in my head. So… yes, it’s distressing in the sense that I don’t like it and I wish it wasn’t happening. I just don’t know how to measure whether it’s “a lot” or whether anxiety is the right word.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty after these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d use the word guilty every time. Sometimes, yes, there’s a feeling afterward that’s like, “Why did I do that?” or “I should have stopped,” but I don’t know if that’s exactly guilt or more like embarrassment or frustration with myself.\n\nIt’s not like I’m thinking I hurt someone else, if that makes sense. It’s more directed inward. Like I feel disappointed in myself, and then I’ll kind of replay it and think about how unnecessary it was, or how I told myself earlier in the night that I wasn’t going to do that again.\n\nSo… maybe guilt is part of it, but I don’t know that I’d say I usually label it that way in my head. It’s more this uncomfortable, self-critical feeling afterward.\n\nDoctor: Do you always feel some level of self-criticism or negative self-thoughts after these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say always. That word makes me hesitate, because I’m sure there are times where I’m just tired or physically uncomfortable and I sort of go to bed without fully thinking it through.\n\nBut most of the time, yes, there’s at least some negative thought afterward. It might not be dramatic every time, like I’m not always spiraling or anything, but there’s usually this little internal commentary of, “Great, you did it again,” or “Why couldn’t you just not do that tonight?” Sometimes it’s quieter, more like a heavy disappointed feeling than actual words.\n\nAnd I guess the criticism can depend on how intense the episode felt. If it was something I could sort of explain to myself as not that bad, I might be less harsh about it. But if it felt really out of control, then afterward I’m much more likely to be frustrated with myself. So not absolutely always, but often enough that it feels like part of the pattern.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience negative self-thoughts after every late-night eating episode?\n\nPatient: I think… no, not every single time. I know I’m sort of splitting hairs, but “every” feels too absolute. There are definitely times where afterward I’m just kind of numb, or tired, or I feel physically uncomfortable and want to go to sleep, and I don’t have a whole clear thought process about it.\n\nBut a lot of the time, yes, something negative creeps in. It might be very brief, like just this little flash of, “Ugh, again,” or feeling disappointed in myself. Other times it’s more drawn out and I’ll lie there thinking about it and criticizing myself. So I wouldn’t say it happens after every episode in the exact same way, but it happens often enough that I associate the late-night eating with that bad feeling afterward.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience negative self-thoughts after most of your late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I think… probably yes, if I’m being honest. Not every single time, like I said, because sometimes I’m just exhausted or kind of shut down afterward, but most of the time there’s something negative there.\n\nIt’s not always this big dramatic thought spiral. Sometimes it’s just a quick, mean little comment in my head, like, “Well, that was stupid,” or “You knew you were going to do this.” Other times it’s more of a feeling than words, like this sinking, disappointed feeling. And then occasionally I do get stuck on it more, where I’m lying there thinking about what I ate and why I couldn’t just stop earlier.\n\nSo… yes, I’d say after most of them, there’s at least some self-critical feeling, even if it’s mild or brief.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have control over what and how much you eat during these late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, that’s probably the part that bothers me the most, honestly. It doesn’t feel like I’m just making a normal choice and then stopping when I’ve had enough.\n\nIt’s more like, once I start, there’s this point where I can feel things shift. At first I might tell myself, okay, I’m just going to have this one thing, or I’ll just eat a little and then be done. But then I keep going back. Like I’ll be sitting there and I’ll think, “You should stop now,” but then I’m already getting up again or opening something else. Or if I’m ordering or looking through food, I’ll keep clicking back to it, adding something, changing it, looking again, even while part of me is thinking, “Why are you doing this?”\n\nSo yes, in that moment it really does feel like the decision has kind of slipped out of my hands. I’m aware of what I’m doing, but I don’t feel fully in control of what or how much. It’s not like I black out or anything. I know what’s happening. But knowing doesn’t seem to stop me once I’m in that mode.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience self-critical thoughts after almost all of your late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Almost all… I don’t know if I’d say that. I think that might be a little too strong, at least the way I’m understanding it.\n\nI know I said it happens most of the time, and that still feels true, but “almost all” makes me think of it being basically every time with only rare exceptions, and I’m not sure I can say that confidently. There are definitely episodes where afterward I’m just kind of blank, or I feel uncomfortable and tired, and I don’t really have a clear self-critical thought in that moment. Or maybe I avoid thinking about it, so I don’t know if that counts.\n\nBut yes, a lot of the time there is something negative afterward. Sometimes it’s very quick, like just a flash of disappointment, and sometimes it sticks around longer. I just wouldn’t want to overstate it and say almost all if that means nearly every single one. I’d feel more accurate saying “most,” or “often,” rather than “almost all.”\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

being honest. All of them? No… I don’t think I can say all. That feels too absolute.

I think I’d need you to clarify what kinds of things you mean, because I don’t want to say yes or no too quickly and give the wrong impression.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after one of these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think about that carefully, because I don’t want to answer too automatically. I don’t really have a specific instance coming to mind where I’ve, like, intentionally done that afterward. Mostly after those nights I’m just sitting with the discomfort and thinking too much about what just happened.\n\nBut I also feel a little thrown by the question, because it’s very direct, and I’m realizing I haven’t really been categorizing my reactions afterward in that kind of way. So… I don’t think that’s something I’ve noticed myself doing, no.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever exercise more than usual the day after one of these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I just don’t know that it turns into actual extra exercise. I’m not someone who’s tracking it closely enough to say, like, yes, every time this happens I go work out harder the next morning.\n\nSo… I haven’t really noticed that, no. At least not in a way that feels intentional or consistent.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over time due to these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m honestly not totally sure. It’s more like I have this general worry that they must be affecting my weight, and then I’ll feel upset about that, but I haven’t tracked it in a way where I could say, “Yes, I gained this much because of those nights.”\n\nSo… I guess the most accurate answer is that I haven’t noticed a clear, significant weight change that I can definitely connect to them.\n\nDoctor: During those late-night eating episodes, do you feel like you're able to stop eating when you want to, or do you feel like you can't stop once you've started?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that part is probably the thing that bothers me the most, honestly. It doesn’t feel like I’m just deciding to have a snack and then deciding to stop. At the beginning, sometimes I can tell myself it’s just going to be one thing, or I’m just going to have a little bit, but then once I’ve started there’s this point where it feels like something shifts.\n\nLike I’ll be sitting there and I know I should stop. I’m not confused about it. I’m literally thinking, “Okay, stop now, you don’t actually need more,” but then I’m still opening something else, or going back to get more, or clicking back into an app to look at food again. It’s almost like I’m watching myself do it from the side, if that makes sense.\n\nAnd I don’t want to make it sound like I’m unconscious or anything. I know what I’m doing. But the decision doesn’t feel fully available to me in that moment. It feels like by the time I realize it’s happening, it’s already kind of… running on its own, and stopping feels much harder than it should.\n\nDoctor: How often do these late-night eating episodes happen?\n\nPatient: It’s been happening pretty regularly, which is hard to say out loud because I think I was kind of telling myself it was more random than it is.\n\nWhen I actually look back at things like delivery receipts, or even just my calendar and what certain nights looked like, there’s usually at least one night every week where it happens. Sometimes it’s more than that. It’s not every single night, but it’s also not like once in a while anymore.\n\nAnd that pattern has been going on for several months, I think. I didn’t really notice it as a “pattern” at first because each time felt like its own separate bad night, but when I look back, it’s pretty consistent.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating once you've started every time one of these late-night eating episodes happens?\n\nPatient: Usually, yes. I’m trying to think if there are exceptions, because I don’t want to exaggerate it and say every single time is exactly the same. There are probably nights where it’s smaller or where I sort of interrupt it earlier.\n\nBut when I think of the episodes I’m actually worried about—the ones I would count as the late-night eating episodes—then yes, the loss of control is kind of the defining part of it. Once I start, there’s this point where I’m watching myself keep going back, like clicking back to the food app, or opening another package, or checking what else is there, even while I’m thinking, “You should stop now.” It’s not like I don’t know I should stop. I do know. That’s what makes it feel so awful.\n\nIt’s more like the decision has already slipped out of my hands by then. I can have the thought to stop, but it doesn’t really translate into stopping. And then afterward I’m like, why didn’t I just close the app or put the food away? But in the moment it feels much harder than that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these late-night eating episodes are causing you a lot of distress or anxiety?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer the “a lot” part. It definitely bothers me, and I don’t feel good about it, especially afterward. But I don’t know if I’d call it anxiety exactly, at least not every time.\n\nIn the moment, I’m usually not sitting there thinking, “I’m anxious.” It’s more like I’m caught up in it, and then afterward I’m uncomfortable with what happened and kind of replay it in my head. So… yes, it’s distressing in the sense that I don’t like it and I wish it wasn’t happening. I just don’t know how to measure whether it’s “a lot” or whether anxiety is the right word.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty after these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d use the word guilty every time. Sometimes, yes, there’s a feeling afterward that’s like, “Why did I do that?” or “I should have stopped,” but I don’t know if that’s exactly guilt or more like embarrassment or frustration with myself.\n\nIt’s not like I’m thinking I hurt someone else, if that makes sense. It’s more directed inward. Like I feel disappointed in myself, and then I’ll kind of replay it and think about how unnecessary it was, or how I told myself earlier in the night that I wasn’t going to do that again.\n\nSo… maybe guilt is part of it, but I don’t know that I’d say I usually label it that way in my head. It’s more this uncomfortable, self-critical feeling afterward.\n\nDoctor: Do you always feel some level of self-criticism or negative self-thoughts after these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say always. That word makes me hesitate, because I’m sure there are times where I’m just tired or physically uncomfortable and I sort of go to bed without fully thinking it through.\n\nBut most of the time, yes, there’s at least some negative thought afterward. It might not be dramatic every time, like I’m not always spiraling or anything, but there’s usually this little internal commentary of, “Great, you did it again,” or “Why couldn’t you just not do that tonight?” Sometimes it’s quieter, more like a heavy disappointed feeling than actual words.\n\nAnd I guess the criticism can depend on how intense the episode felt. I mean, I notice it afterward, definitely, and I’ll kind of think back over what I ate and feel… I don’t know, uneasy about it maybe? But I’m not sure if I’d call it guilt exactly, or if it’s more that I’m confused and frustrated that it happened again.\n\nIt’s hard because in the moment I’m not really analyzing it, and afterward I’m sort of just sitting there aware that it was a lot. So… maybe there’s some self-judgment in there, but I don’t know if “guilty” is the exact word I’d use.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever do anything to try to make up for or counteract the extra calories you consume on those late nights?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that, because I don’t know exactly what would “count,” if that makes sense. Like, afterward I’m usually more in this mode of thinking, okay, that was too much, I wish I hadn’t done that, and then I sort of mentally promise myself I’ll be more normal about food the next day.\n\nBut I haven’t really kept track of whether I’m actively doing something to balance it out, versus just feeling uncomfortable and wanting to reset somehow. I think I’d need you to clarify what kinds of things you mean, because I don’t want to say yes or no too quickly and give the wrong impression.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after one of these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think about that carefully, because I don’t want to answer too automatically. I don’t really have a specific instance coming to mind where I’ve, like, intentionally done that afterward. Mostly after those nights I’m just sitting with the discomfort and thinking too much about what just happened.\n\nBut I also feel a little thrown by the question, because it’s very direct, and I’m realizing I haven’t really been categorizing my reactions afterward in that kind of way. So… I don’t think that’s something I’ve noticed myself doing, no.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever exercise more than usual the day after one of these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I don’t think I have a clear pattern where the next day I deliberately go, “Okay, I need to exercise because of what I ate last night.” That doesn’t really sound like something I’ve been consciously doing.\n\nBut I do sometimes have that feeling the next day of wanting to be more “on track,” if that makes sense, and I might think vaguely that I should move more or be healthier. I just don’t know that it turns into actual extra exercise. I’m not someone who’s tracking it closely enough to say, like, yes, every time this happens I go work out harder the next morning.\n\nSo… I haven’t really noticed that, no. At least not in a way that feels intentional or consistent.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over time due to these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m honestly not totally sure. I don’t weigh myself very consistently, partly because it can make me feel worse or get kind of stuck in my head about it, so I don’t have a clear before-and-after number I can point to.\n\nI do sometimes feel like my body changes a little, or like my clothes fit differently at certain times, but I can’t say confidently that it’s because of the late-night eating episodes specifically. It’s more like I have this general worry that they must be affecting my weight, and then I’ll feel upset about that, but I haven’t tracked it in a way where I could say, “Yes, I gained this much because of those nights.”\n\nSo… I guess the most accurate answer is that I haven’t noticed a clear, significant weight change that I can definitely connect to them.\n\nDoctor: During those late-night eating episodes, do you feel like you're able to stop eating when you want to, or do you feel like you can't stop once you've started?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that part is probably the thing that bothers me the most, honestly. It doesn’t feel like I’m just deciding to have a snack and then deciding to stop. At the beginning, sometimes I can tell myself it’s just going to be one thing, or I’m just going to have a little bit, but then once I’ve started there’s this point where it feels like something shifts.\n\nLike I’ll be sitting there and I know I should stop. I’m not confused about it. I’m literally thinking, “Okay, stop now, you don’t actually need more,” but then I’m still opening something else, or going back to get more, or clicking back into an app to look at food again. It’s almost like I’m watching myself do it from the side, if that makes sense.\n\nAnd I don’t want to make it sound like I’m unconscious or anything. I know what I’m doing. But the decision doesn’t feel fully available to me in that moment. It feels like by the time I realize it’s happening, it’s already kind of… running on its own, and stopping feels much harder than it should.\n\nDoctor: How often do these late-night eating episodes happen?\n\nPatient: It’s been happening pretty regularly, which is hard to say out loud because I think I was kind of telling myself it was more random than it is.\n\nWhen I actually look back at things like delivery receipts, or even just my calendar and what certain nights looked like, there’s usually at least one night every week where it happens. Sometimes it’s more than that. It’s not every single night, but it’s also not like once in a while anymore.\n\nAnd that pattern has been going on for several months, I think. I didn’t really notice it as a “pattern” at first because each time felt like its own separate bad night, but when I look back, it’s pretty consistent.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating once you've started every time one of these late-night eating episodes happens?\n\nPatient: Usually, yes. I’m trying to think if there are exceptions, because I don’t want to exaggerate it and say every single time is exactly the same. There are probably nights where it’s smaller or where I sort of interrupt it earlier.\n\nBut when I think of the episodes I’m actually worried about—the ones I would count as the late-night eating episodes—then yes, the loss of control is kind of the defining part of it. Once I start, there’s this point where I’m watching myself keep going back, like clicking back to the food app, or opening another package, or checking what else is there, even while I’m thinking, “You should stop now.” It’s not like I don’t know I should stop. I do know. That’s what makes it feel so awful.\n\nIt’s more like the decision has already slipped out of my hands by then. I can have the thought to stop, but it doesn’t really translate into stopping. And then afterward I’m like, why didn’t I just close the app or put the food away? But in the moment it feels much harder than that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these late-night eating episodes are causing you a lot of distress or anxiety?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer the “a lot” part. It definitely bothers me, and I don’t feel good about it, especially afterward. But I don’t know if I’d call it anxiety exactly, at least not every time.\n\nIn the moment, I’m usually not sitting there thinking, “I’m anxious.” It’s more like I’m caught up in it, and then afterward I’m uncomfortable with what happened and kind of replay it in my head. So… yes, it’s distressing in the sense that I don’t like it and I wish it wasn’t happening. I just don’t know how to measure whether it’s “a lot” or whether anxiety is the right word.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty after these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d use the word guilty every time. Sometimes, yes, there’s a feeling afterward that’s like, “Why did I do that?” or “I should have stopped,” but I don’t know if that’s exactly guilt or more like embarrassment or frustration with myself.\n\nIt’s not like I’m thinking I hurt someone else, if that makes sense. It’s more directed inward. Like I feel disappointed in myself, and then I’ll kind of replay it and think about how unnecessary it was, or how I told myself earlier in the night that I wasn’t going to do that again.\n\nSo… maybe guilt is part of it, but I don’t know that I’d say I usually label it that way in my head. It’s more this uncomfortable, self-critical feeling afterward.\n\nDoctor: Do you always feel some level of self-criticism or negative self-thoughts after these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say always. That word makes me hesitate, because I’m sure there are times where I’m just tired or physically uncomfortable and I sort of go to bed without fully thinking it through.\n\nBut most of the time, yes, there’s at least some negative thought afterward. It might not be dramatic every time, like I’m not always spiraling or anything, but there’s usually this little internal commentary of, “Great, you did it again,” or “Why couldn’t you just not do that tonight?” Sometimes it’s quieter, more like a heavy disappointed feeling than actual words.\n\nAnd I guess the criticism can depend on how intense the episode felt. If it was something I could sort of explain to myself as not that bad, I might be less harsh about it. But if it felt really out of control, then afterward I’m much more likely to be frustrated with myself. So not absolutely always, but often enough that it feels like part of the pattern.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience negative self-thoughts after every late-night eating episode?\n\nPatient: I think… no, not every single time. I know I’m sort of splitting hairs, but “every” feels too absolute. There are definitely times where afterward I’m just kind of numb, or tired, or I feel physically uncomfortable and want to go to sleep, and I don’t have a whole clear thought process about it.\n\nBut a lot of the time, yes, something negative creeps in. It might be very brief, like just this little flash of, “Ugh, again,” or feeling disappointed in myself. Other times it’s more drawn out and I’ll lie there thinking about it and criticizing myself. So I wouldn’t say it happens after every episode in the exact same way, but it happens often enough that I associate the late-night eating with that bad feeling afterward.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience negative self-thoughts after most of your late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I think… probably yes, if I’m being honest. Not every single time, like I said, because sometimes I’m just exhausted or kind of shut down afterward, but most of the time there’s something negative there.\n\nIt’s not always this big dramatic thought spiral. Sometimes it’s just a quick, mean little comment in my head, like, “Well, that was stupid,” or “You knew you were going to do this.” Other times it’s more of a feeling than words, like this sinking, disappointed feeling. And then occasionally I do get stuck on it more, where I’m lying there thinking about what I ate and why I couldn’t just stop earlier.\n\nSo… yes, I’d say after most of them, there’s at least some self-critical feeling, even if it’s mild or brief.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have control over what and how much you eat during these late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, that’s probably the part that bothers me the most, honestly. I’m not totally sure how to answer that, because “guilty” is one of those words that feels close, but I don’t know if it’s always exactly that.

Sometimes, yes, I do feel bad afterward, and it can kind of turn into this broader feeling about myself, like I’m disappointed in myself or frustrated that I ended up doing the same thing again. But I don’t know if I’d say it usually reaches the point where it changes my whole sense of who I am, if that’s what you mean. It’s more like… in that moment, I feel worse about myself. I might think, “Why couldn’t you just stop?” or feel embarrassed even though no one else saw it.

But I’m also trying not to overstate it. There are times when I mostly feel physically uncomfortable or tired, and I don’t really go into a big guilt spiral. Other times I try not to think about it at all. So I guess my answer is: often there’s some guilt or shame-ish feeling afterward, but I’m not sure I’d confidently say “usually” or that it always affects how I feel about myself in a deep way.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.8, F50.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess what finally made me come in is… my eating has started to feel really out of hand, especially on nights when I work late. I’m a graphic designer, so sometimes I’m just sitting there for hours trying to finish something, and I’ll order what I tell myself is just dinner. But then it ends up being, like, a full entrée, sides, dessert, and then afterward I’m still going into my kitchen for extra snacks.\n\nAnd it’s not really the amount I’d normally eat, if that makes sense. Like, if I were having dinner with someone sitting across from me, I would never order or eat that much. It feels like way more than a normal meal for me, and it’s been bothering me enough that I thought I should talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty after eating these large amounts of food late at night?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I mean, I notice it afterward, definitely, and I’ll kind of think back over what I ate and feel… I don’t know, uneasy about it maybe? But I’m not sure if I’d call it guilt exactly, or if it’s more that I’m confused and frustrated that it happened again.\n\nIt’s hard because in the moment I’m not really analyzing it, and afterward I’m sort of just sitting there aware that it was a lot. So… maybe there’s some self-judgment in there, but I don’t know if “guilty” is the exact word I’d use.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever do anything to try to make up for or counteract the extra calories you consume on those late nights?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that, because I don’t know exactly what would “count,” if that makes sense. Like, afterward I’m usually more in this mode of thinking, okay, that was too much, I wish I hadn’t done that, and then I sort of mentally promise myself I’ll be more normal about food the next day.\n\nBut I haven’t really kept track of whether I’m actively doing something to balance it out, versus just feeling uncomfortable and wanting to reset somehow. I think I’d need you to clarify what kinds of things you mean, because I don’t want to say yes or no too quickly and give the wrong impression.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after one of these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think about that carefully, because I don’t want to answer too automatically. I don’t really have a specific instance coming to mind where I’ve, like, intentionally done that afterward. Mostly after those nights I’m just sitting with the discomfort and thinking too much about what just happened.\n\nBut I also feel a little thrown by the question, because it’s very direct, and I’m realizing I haven’t really been categorizing my reactions afterward in that kind of way. So… I don’t think that’s something I’ve noticed myself doing, no.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever exercise more than usual the day after one of these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I don’t think I have a clear pattern where the next day I deliberately go, “Okay, I need to exercise because of what I ate last night.” That doesn’t really sound like something I’ve been consciously doing.\n\nBut I do sometimes have that feeling the next day of wanting to be more “on track,” if that makes sense, and I might think vaguely that I should move more or be healthier. I just don’t know that it turns into actual extra exercise. I’m not someone who’s tracking it closely enough to say, like, yes, every time this happens I go work out harder the next morning.\n\nSo… I haven’t really noticed that, no. At least not in a way that feels intentional or consistent.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over time due to these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m honestly not totally sure. I don’t weigh myself very consistently, partly because it can make me feel worse or get kind of stuck in my head about it, so I don’t have a clear before-and-after number I can point to.\n\nI do sometimes feel like my body changes a little, or like my clothes fit differently at certain times, but I can’t say confidently that it’s because of the late-night eating episodes specifically. It’s more like I have this general worry that they must be affecting my weight, and then I’ll feel upset about that, but I haven’t tracked it in a way where I could say, “Yes, I gained this much because of those nights.”\n\nSo… I guess the most accurate answer is that I haven’t noticed a clear, significant weight change that I can definitely connect to them.\n\nDoctor: During those late-night eating episodes, do you feel like you're able to stop eating when you want to, or do you feel like you can't stop once you've started?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that part is probably the thing that bothers me the most, honestly. It doesn’t feel like I’m just deciding to have a snack and then deciding to stop. At the beginning, sometimes I can tell myself it’s just going to be one thing, or I’m just going to have a little bit, but then once I’ve started there’s this point where it feels like something shifts.\n\nLike I’ll be sitting there and I know I should stop. I’m not confused about it. I’m literally thinking, “Okay, stop now, you don’t actually need more,” but then I’m still opening something else, or going back to get more, or clicking back into an app to look at food again. It’s almost like I’m watching myself do it from the side, if that makes sense.\n\nAnd I don’t want to make it sound like I’m unconscious or anything. I know what I’m doing. But the decision doesn’t feel fully available to me in that moment. It feels like by the time I realize it’s happening, it’s already kind of… running on its own, and stopping feels much harder than it should.\n\nDoctor: How often do these late-night eating episodes happen?\n\nPatient: It’s been happening pretty regularly, which is hard to say out loud because I think I was kind of telling myself it was more random than it is.\n\nWhen I actually look back at things like delivery receipts, or even just my calendar and what certain nights looked like, there’s usually at least one night every week where it happens. Sometimes it’s more than that. It’s not every single night, but it’s also not like once in a while anymore.\n\nAnd that pattern has been going on for several months, I think. I didn’t really notice it as a “pattern” at first because each time felt like its own separate bad night, but when I look back, it’s pretty consistent.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating once you've started every time one of these late-night eating episodes happens?\n\nPatient: Usually, yes. I’m trying to think if there are exceptions, because I don’t want to exaggerate it and say every single time is exactly the same. There are probably nights where it’s smaller or where I sort of interrupt it earlier.\n\nBut when I think of the episodes I’m actually worried about—the ones I would count as the late-night eating episodes—then yes, the loss of control is kind of the defining part of it. Once I start, there’s this point where I’m watching myself keep going back, like clicking back to the food app, or opening another package, or checking what else is there, even while I’m thinking, “You should stop now.” It’s not like I don’t know I should stop. I do know. That’s what makes it feel so awful.\n\nIt’s more like the decision has already slipped out of my hands by then. I can have the thought to stop, but it doesn’t really translate into stopping. And then afterward I’m like, why didn’t I just close the app or put the food away? But in the moment it feels much harder than that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these late-night eating episodes are causing you a lot of distress or anxiety?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer the “a lot” part. It definitely bothers me, and I don’t feel good about it, especially afterward. But I don’t know if I’d call it anxiety exactly, at least not every time.\n\nIn the moment, I’m usually not sitting there thinking, “I’m anxious.” It’s more like I’m caught up in it, and then afterward I’m uncomfortable with what happened and kind of replay it in my head. So… yes, it’s distressing in the sense that I don’t like it and I wish it wasn’t happening. I just don’t know how to measure whether it’s “a lot” or whether anxiety is the right word.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty after these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d use the word guilty every time. Sometimes, yes, there’s a feeling afterward that’s like, “Why did I do that?” or “I should have stopped,” but I don’t know if that’s exactly guilt or more like embarrassment or frustration with myself.\n\nIt’s not like I’m thinking I hurt someone else, if that makes sense. It’s more directed inward. Like I feel disappointed in myself, and then I’ll kind of replay it and think about how unnecessary it was, or how I told myself earlier in the night that I wasn’t going to do that again.\n\nSo… maybe guilt is part of it, but I don’t know that I’d say I usually label it that way in my head. It’s more this uncomfortable, self-critical feeling afterward.\n\nDoctor: Do you always feel some level of self-criticism or negative self-thoughts after these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say always. That word makes me hesitate, because I’m sure there are times where I’m just tired or physically uncomfortable and I sort of go to bed without fully thinking it through.\n\nBut most of the time, yes, there’s at least some negative thought afterward. It might not be dramatic every time, like I’m not always spiraling or anything, but there’s usually this little internal commentary of, “Great, you did it again,” or “Why couldn’t you just not do that tonight?” Sometimes it’s quieter, more like a heavy disappointed feeling than actual words.\n\nAnd I guess the criticism can depend on how intense the episode felt. If it was something I could sort of explain to myself as not that bad, I might be less harsh about it. But if it felt really out of control, then afterward I’m much more likely to be frustrated with myself. So not absolutely always, but often enough that it feels like part of the pattern.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience negative self-thoughts after every late-night eating episode?\n\nPatient: I think… no, not every single time. I know I’m sort of splitting hairs, but “every” feels too absolute. There are definitely times where afterward I’m just kind of numb, or tired, or I feel physically uncomfortable and want to go to sleep, and I don’t have a whole clear thought process about it.\n\nBut a lot of the time, yes, something negative creeps in. It might be very brief, like just this little flash of, “Ugh, again,” or feeling disappointed in myself. Other times it’s more drawn out and I’ll lie there thinking about it and criticizing myself. So I wouldn’t say it happens after every episode in the exact same way, but it happens often enough that I associate the late-night eating with that bad feeling afterward.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience negative self-thoughts after most of your late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I think… probably yes, if I’m being honest. Not every single time, like I said, because sometimes I’m just exhausted or kind of shut down afterward, but most of the time there’s something negative there.\n\nIt’s not always this big dramatic thought spiral. Sometimes it’s just a quick, mean little comment in my head, like, “Well, that was stupid,” or “You knew you were going to do this.” Other times it’s more of a feeling than words, like this sinking, disappointed feeling. And then occasionally I do get stuck on it more, where I’m lying there thinking about what I ate and why I couldn’t just stop earlier.\n\nSo… yes, I’d say after most of them, there’s at least some self-critical feeling, even if it’s mild or brief.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have control over what and how much you eat during these late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, that’s probably the part that bothers me the most, honestly. It doesn’t feel like I’m just making a normal choice and then stopping when I’ve had enough.\n\nIt’s more like, once I start, there’s this point where I can feel things shift. At first I might tell myself, okay, I’m just going to have this one thing, or I’ll just eat a little and then be done. But then I keep going back. Like I’ll be sitting there and I’ll think, “You should stop now,” but then I’m already getting up again or opening something else. Or if I’m ordering or looking through food, I’ll keep clicking back to it, adding something, changing it, looking again, even while part of me is thinking, “Why are you doing this?”\n\nSo yes, in that moment it really does feel like the decision has kind of slipped out of my hands. I’m aware of what I’m doing, but I don’t feel fully in control of what or how much. It’s not like I black out or anything. I know what’s happening. But knowing doesn’t seem to stop me once I’m in that mode.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience self-critical thoughts after almost all of your late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Almost all… I don’t know if I’d say that. I think that might be a little too strong, at least the way I’m understanding it.\n\nI know I said it happens most of the time, and that still feels true, but “almost all” makes me think of it being basically every time with only rare exceptions, and I’m not sure I can say that confidently. There are definitely episodes where afterward I’m just kind of blank, or I feel uncomfortable and tired, and I don’t really have a clear self-critical thought in that moment. Or maybe I avoid thinking about it, so I don’t know if that counts.\n\nBut yes, a lot of the time there is something negative afterward. Sometimes it’s very quick, like just a flash of disappointment, and sometimes it sticks around longer. I just wouldn’t want to overstate it and say almost all if that means nearly every single one. I’d feel more accurate saying “most,” or “often,” rather than “almost all.”\n\nDoctor: Do you experience self-critical thoughts after all of your late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: All of them? No… I don’t think I can say all. That feels too absolute.\n\nI know I’ve said there’s usually something afterward, and that’s true, but there are times when I don’t really have a clear thought like that. Sometimes I’m just tired, or sort of numb, or I go straight into distracting myself so I don’t have to sit with it. And I guess maybe someone could argue that avoiding it means there’s something negative underneath, but if you’re asking whether I consciously think self-critical things after every single episode, then no, not every single time.\n\nMore often than not, yes, there’s disappointment or that kind of “why did you do that again” feeling. But I wouldn’t say it happens after all of them.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty after these episodes, to the point where it affects how you feel about yourself?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, because “guilty” is one of those words that feels close, but I don’t know if it’s always exactly that.\n\nSometimes, yes, I do feel bad afterward, and it can kind of turn into this broader feeling about myself, like I’m disappointed in myself or frustrated that I ended up doing the same thing again. But I don’t know if I’d say it usually reaches the point where it changes my whole sense of who I am, if that’s what you mean. It’s more like… in that moment, I feel worse about myself. I might think, “Why couldn’t you just stop?” or feel embarrassed even though no one else saw it.\n\nBut I’m also trying not to overstate it. There are times when I mostly feel physically uncomfortable or tired, and I don’t really go into a big guilt spiral. Other times I try not to think about it at all. So I guess my answer is: often there’s some guilt or shame-ish feeling afterward, but I’m not sure I’d confidently say “usually” or that it always affects how I feel about myself in a deep way.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m honestly not completely sure how to separate that out. I know I feel bad afterward a lot of the time, like disappointed in myself or embarrassed, even if nobody else knows it happened. But “significantly affects my self-esteem” feels like a bigger statement, and I don’t know if I can confidently say that every time or even in a really clear way.

Sometimes it’s more like a sharp thought in the moment, like, “What is wrong with me?” or “Why did I do that again?” And that does feel personal, not just like, “Oh, I ate too much.” It can make me feel kind of weak or out of control, which I don’t like admitting. But other times I’m just tired or uncomfortable and I kind of shut it down mentally because I don’t want to sit there analyzing myself at midnight.

So… yes, there can be guilt and self-critical thoughts, and sometimes they do make me feel worse about myself. But if you mean does it consistently or deeply damage my self-esteem in a significant way after each episode, I don’t know that I can say that. I think it varies, and I may not always be fully aware of how much it affects me.

It feels like by the time I realize it’s happening, it’s already kind of… running on its own, and stopping feels much harder than it should.\n\nDoctor: How often do these late-night eating episodes happen?\n\nPatient: It’s been happening pretty regularly, which is hard to say out loud because I think I was kind of telling myself it was more random than it is.\n\nWhen I actually look back at things like delivery receipts, or even just my calendar and what certain nights looked like, there’s usually at least one night every week where it happens. Sometimes it’s more than that. It’s not every single night, but it’s also not like once in a while anymore.\n\nAnd that pattern has been going on for several months, I think. I didn’t really notice it as a “pattern” at first because each time felt like its own separate bad night, but when I look back, it’s pretty consistent.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating once you've started every time one of these late-night eating episodes happens?\n\nPatient: Usually, yes. I’m trying to think if there are exceptions, because I don’t want to exaggerate it and say every single time is exactly the same. There are probably nights where it’s smaller or where I sort of interrupt it earlier.\n\nBut when I think of the episodes I’m actually worried about—the ones I would count as the late-night eating episodes—then yes, the loss of control is kind of the defining part of it. Once I start, there’s this point where I’m watching myself keep going back, like clicking back to the food app, or opening another package, or checking what else is there, even while I’m thinking, “You should stop now.” It’s not like I don’t know I should stop. I do know. That’s what makes it feel so awful.\n\nIt’s more like the decision has already slipped out of my hands by then. I can have the thought to stop, but it doesn’t really translate into stopping. And then afterward I’m like, why didn’t I just close the app or put the food away? But in the moment it feels much harder than that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these late-night eating episodes are causing you a lot of distress or anxiety?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer the “a lot” part. It definitely bothers me, and I don’t feel good about it, especially afterward. But I don’t know if I’d call it anxiety exactly, at least not every time.\n\nIn the moment, I’m usually not sitting there thinking, “I’m anxious.” It’s more like I’m caught up in it, and then afterward I’m uncomfortable with what happened and kind of replay it in my head. So… yes, it’s distressing in the sense that I don’t like it and I wish it wasn’t happening. I just don’t know how to measure whether it’s “a lot” or whether anxiety is the right word.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty after these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d use the word guilty every time. Sometimes, yes, there’s a feeling afterward that’s like, “Why did I do that?” or “I should have stopped,” but I don’t know if that’s exactly guilt or more like embarrassment or frustration with myself.\n\nIt’s not like I’m thinking I hurt someone else, if that makes sense. It’s more directed inward. Like I feel disappointed in myself, and then I’ll kind of replay it and think about how unnecessary it was, or how I told myself earlier in the night that I wasn’t going to do that again.\n\nSo… maybe guilt is part of it, but I don’t know that I’d say I usually label it that way in my head. It’s more this uncomfortable, self-critical feeling afterward.\n\nDoctor: Do you always feel some level of self-criticism or negative self-thoughts after these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say always. That word makes me hesitate, because I’m sure there are times where I’m just tired or physically uncomfortable and I sort of go to bed without fully thinking it through.\n\nBut most of the time, yes, there’s at least some negative thought afterward. It might not be dramatic every time, like I’m not always spiraling or anything, but there’s usually this little internal commentary of, “Great, you did it again,” or “Why couldn’t you just not do that tonight?” Sometimes it’s quieter, more like a heavy disappointed feeling than actual words.\n\nAnd I guess the criticism can depend on how intense the episode felt. If it was something I could sort of explain to myself as not that bad, I might be less harsh about it. But if it felt really out of control, then afterward I’m much more likely to be frustrated with myself. So not absolutely always, but often enough that it feels like part of the pattern.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience negative self-thoughts after every late-night eating episode?\n\nPatient: I think… no, not every single time. I know I’m sort of splitting hairs, but “every” feels too absolute. There are definitely times where afterward I’m just kind of numb, or tired, or I feel physically uncomfortable and want to go to sleep, and I don’t have a whole clear thought process about it.\n\nBut a lot of the time, yes, something negative creeps in. It might be very brief, like just this little flash of, “Ugh, again,” or feeling disappointed in myself. Other times it’s more drawn out and I’ll lie there thinking about it and criticizing myself. So I wouldn’t say it happens after every episode in the exact same way, but it happens often enough that I associate the late-night eating with that bad feeling afterward.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience negative self-thoughts after most of your late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I think… probably yes, if I’m being honest. Not every single time, like I said, because sometimes I’m just exhausted or kind of shut down afterward, but most of the time there’s something negative there.\n\nIt’s not always this big dramatic thought spiral. Sometimes it’s just a quick, mean little comment in my head, like, “Well, that was stupid,” or “You knew you were going to do this.” Other times it’s more of a feeling than words, like this sinking, disappointed feeling. And then occasionally I do get stuck on it more, where I’m lying there thinking about what I ate and why I couldn’t just stop earlier.\n\nSo… yes, I’d say after most of them, there’s at least some self-critical feeling, even if it’s mild or brief.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have control over what and how much you eat during these late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, that’s probably the part that bothers me the most, honestly. It doesn’t feel like I’m just making a normal choice and then stopping when I’ve had enough.\n\nIt’s more like, once I start, there’s this point where I can feel things shift. At first I might tell myself, okay, I’m just going to have this one thing, or I’ll just eat a little and then be done. But then I keep going back. Like I’ll be sitting there and I’ll think, “You should stop now,” but then I’m already getting up again or opening something else. Or if I’m ordering or looking through food, I’ll keep clicking back to it, adding something, changing it, looking again, even while part of me is thinking, “Why are you doing this?”\n\nSo yes, in that moment it really does feel like the decision has kind of slipped out of my hands. I’m aware of what I’m doing, but I don’t feel fully in control of what or how much. It’s not like I black out or anything. I know what’s happening. But knowing doesn’t seem to stop me once I’m in that mode.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience self-critical thoughts after almost all of your late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Almost all… I don’t know if I’d say that. I think that might be a little too strong, at least the way I’m understanding it.\n\nI know I said it happens most of the time, and that still feels true, but “almost all” makes me think of it being basically every time with only rare exceptions, and I’m not sure I can say that confidently. There are definitely episodes where afterward I’m just kind of blank, or I feel uncomfortable and tired, and I don’t really have a clear self-critical thought in that moment. Or maybe I avoid thinking about it, so I don’t know if that counts.\n\nBut yes, a lot of the time there is something negative afterward. Sometimes it’s very quick, like just a flash of disappointment, and sometimes it sticks around longer. I just wouldn’t want to overstate it and say almost all if that means nearly every single one. I’d feel more accurate saying “most,” or “often,” rather than “almost all.”\n\nDoctor: Do you experience self-critical thoughts after all of your late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: All of them? No… I don’t think I can say all. That feels too absolute.\n\nI know I’ve said there’s usually something afterward, and that’s true, but there are times when I don’t really have a clear thought like that. Sometimes I’m just tired, or sort of numb, or I go straight into distracting myself so I don’t have to sit with it. And I guess maybe someone could argue that avoiding it means there’s something negative underneath, but if you’re asking whether I consciously think self-critical things after every single episode, then no, not every single time.\n\nMore often than not, yes, there’s disappointment or that kind of “why did you do that again” feeling. But I wouldn’t say it happens after all of them.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty after these episodes, to the point where it affects how you feel about yourself?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, because “guilty” is one of those words that feels close, but I don’t know if it’s always exactly that.\n\nSometimes, yes, I do feel bad afterward, and it can kind of turn into this broader feeling about myself, like I’m disappointed in myself or frustrated that I ended up doing the same thing again. But I don’t know if I’d say it usually reaches the point where it changes my whole sense of who I am, if that’s what you mean. It’s more like… in that moment, I feel worse about myself. I might think, “Why couldn’t you just stop?” or feel embarrassed even though no one else saw it.\n\nBut I’m also trying not to overstate it. There are times when I mostly feel physically uncomfortable or tired, and I don’t really go into a big guilt spiral. Other times I try not to think about it at all. So I guess my answer is: often there’s some guilt or shame-ish feeling afterward, but I’m not sure I’d confidently say “usually” or that it always affects how I feel about myself in a deep way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or self-critical after these late-night eating episodes to the point where it significantly affects your self-esteem?\n\nPatient: I’m honestly not completely sure how to separate that out. I know I feel bad afterward a lot of the time, like disappointed in myself or embarrassed, even if nobody else knows it happened. But “significantly affects my self-esteem” feels like a bigger statement, and I don’t know if I can confidently say that every time or even in a really clear way.\n\nSometimes it’s more like a sharp thought in the moment, like, “What is wrong with me?” or “Why did I do that again?” And that does feel personal, not just like, “Oh, I ate too much.” It can make me feel kind of weak or out of control, which I don’t like admitting. But other times I’m just tired or uncomfortable and I kind of shut it down mentally because I don’t want to sit there analyzing myself at midnight.\n\nSo… yes, there can be guilt and self-critical thoughts, and sometimes they do make me feel worse about myself. But if you mean does it consistently or deeply damage my self-esteem in a significant way after each episode, I don’t know that I can say that. I think it varies, and I may not always be fully aware of how much it affects me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t love saying it that plainly, but when I actually look back instead of just guessing, it does seem like at least once a week.

But then it ends up being, like, a full entrée, sides, dessert, and then afterward I’m still going into my kitchen for extra snacks.\n\nAnd it’s not really the amount I’d normally eat, if that makes sense.
